========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess what brings me in is that I’ve had this low feeling hanging around for a long time now. I still get up, I still do my routes, I still show up for my volunteer shifts and do what I’m supposed to do, but most days it feels like I’m just kind of moving through it all on autopilot.

It’s not like I’m falling apart in some obvious way, I don’t think. From the outside I probably look like I’m managing. But inside it’s just this heavy, flat kind of feeling, like I’m not really in my own life the way I used to be. I’m getting things done, but I don’t feel very present in any of it. That’s probably the best way I can explain it.

I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the same things I used to do, and I can’t say I never enjoy anything at all, but it’s harder for me to tell what “pleasure” is supposed to feel like anymore, if that makes sense.

It’s been… about three years, I’d say. It started after my father died, or at least that’s when I first really noticed it settling in.

I guess I’m still not sure I’d call it “lost interest,” exactly. I haven’t stopped doing everything I used to do, and there are still moments where something feels okay or worthwhile. It’s more like the feeling I get from things is dulled down.

Like, if I do something I used to enjoy, I might still show up and participate, and afterward I can say, “Yeah, that was fine,” but I don’t always have that same pull toward it or that easy enjoyment while I’m doing it. Sometimes I’m doing it because it’s part of my routine, or because I know it’s probably better than doing nothing, rather than because I’m really looking forward to it.

So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have zero interest, but it doesn’t feel the same as it used to. It’s kind of muted, I guess.

I figured it was just part of dealing with him being gone, and that it would lift after a while. But it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed there in the background, and then over time it became hard to tell where the grief ended and where this ongoing low mood began. So, yeah… roughly three years now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not sure I’d call it “lost interest,” exactly. I haven’t stopped doing everything I used to do, and there are still moments where something feels okay or worthwhile. It’s more like the feeling I get from things is dulled down.\n\nLike, if I do something I used to enjoy, I might still show up and participate, and afterward I can say, “Yeah, that was fine,” but I don’t always have that same pull toward it or that easy enjoyment while I’m doing it. Sometimes I’m doing it because it’s part of my routine, or because I know it’s probably better than doing nothing, rather than because I’m really looking forward to it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have zero interest, but it doesn’t feel the same as it used to. It’s kind of muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve clearly noticed, no. I mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little like anyone’s does, depending on what I’m eating or how busy I am with work, but I can’t point to some major change over the last three years.\n\nI haven’t had to buy a whole different size of clothes or anything like that, and nobody’s really commented on it to me. Yeah, I have noticed that some, especially around work. It’s not every single night, but on nights before I’ve got a run the next day, I’ll get into bed and then my mind starts going over all the stops, what time I need to be where, whether traffic’s going to be bad, whether I’m forgetting something… just all that stuff.

Sometimes I do fall asleep eventually, but then I’ll wake up around four in the morning, maybe even a little before, and once I’m awake it’s like my brain turns back on. I might still lie there for another hour or two, but I’m not really sleeping again. I’m just kind of watching the clock and thinking, well, if I fall asleep now I’ll get this much time, and then that makes it worse.

I still usually make it to work on time, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m managing it okay. But those mornings, I feel like I’m already starting the day worn down before I’ve even gotten in the truck.

It’s more like the feeling I get from things is dulled down.\n\nLike, if I do something I used to enjoy, I might still show up and participate, and afterward I can say, “Yeah, that was fine,” but I don’t always have that same pull toward it or that easy enjoyment while I’m doing it. Sometimes I’m doing it because it’s part of my routine, or because I know it’s probably better than doing nothing, rather than because I’m really looking forward to it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have zero interest, but it doesn’t feel the same as it used to. It’s kind of muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve clearly noticed, no. I mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little like anyone’s does, depending on what I’m eating or how busy I am with work, but I can’t point to some major change over the last three years.\n\nI haven’t had to buy a whole different size of clothes or anything like that, and nobody’s really commented on it to me. I don’t weigh myself all the time, so I couldn’t give you an exact number, but from what I can tell, it’s been more or less the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have noticed that some, especially around work. It’s not every single night, but on nights before I’ve got a run the next day, I’ll get into bed and then my mind starts going over all the stops, what time I need to be where, whether traffic’s going to be bad, whether I’m forgetting something… just all that stuff.\n\nSometimes I do fall asleep eventually, but then I’ll wake up around four in the morning, maybe even a little before, and once I’m awake it’s like my brain turns back on. I might still lie there for another hour or two, but I’m not really sleeping again. I’m just kind of watching the clock and thinking, well, if I fall asleep now I’ll get this much time, and then that makes it worse.\n\nI still usually make it to work on time, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m managing it okay. But those mornings, I feel like I’m already starting the day worn down before I’ve even gotten in the truck.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’d say I’m completely unable to feel pleasure, no. That sounds a little too absolute for what it’s like for me.

It’s more that it takes more for something to feel enjoyable, or the enjoyment doesn’t last as long. Like I can still have moments where I laugh at something, or I’m glad I did something instead of just sitting around. But a lot of the time, things I used to look forward to feel more like… I don’t know, just something to get through, or something I do because I know I should.

So if you mean, do I never enjoy anything anymore, then no, that’s not exactly it. But if you mean whether the enjoyment feels kind of weaker or harder to get to than it used to, then yeah, I guess that’s closer.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is that I’ve had this low feeling hanging around for a long time now. I still get up, I still do my routes, I still show up for my volunteer shifts and do what I’m supposed to do, but most days it feels like I’m just kind of moving through it all on autopilot.\n\nIt’s not like I’m falling apart in some obvious way, I don’t think. From the outside I probably look like I’m managing. But inside it’s just this heavy, flat kind of feeling, like I’m not really in my own life the way I used to be. I’m getting things done, but I don’t feel very present in any of it. That’s probably the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the same things I used to do, and I can’t say I never enjoy anything at all, but it’s harder for me to tell what “pleasure” is supposed to feel like anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll go through the motions with things, and sometimes I’ll think, okay, that was fine, or I’m glad I did it instead of just sitting around. But it’s not always that same kind of easy enjoyment I remember having before. I guess I haven’t really sat down and measured it, you know? It’s more like I notice after the fact that things feel kind of muted. But I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely lost interest or anything that clear-cut.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this low feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three years, I’d say. It started after my father died, or at least that’s when I first really noticed it settling in.\n\nAt the time I thought, well, of course I feel bad, you know? That made sense. I figured it was just part of dealing with him being gone, and that it would lift after a while. But it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed there in the background, and then over time it became hard to tell where the grief ended and where this ongoing low mood began. So, yeah… roughly three years now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not sure I’d call it “lost interest,” exactly. I haven’t stopped doing everything I used to do, and there are still moments where something feels okay or worthwhile. It’s more like the feeling I get from things is dulled down.\n\nLike, if I do something I used to enjoy, I might still show up and participate, and afterward I can say, “Yeah, that was fine,” but I don’t always have that same pull toward it or that easy enjoyment while I’m doing it. I don’t weigh myself all the time, so I couldn’t give you an exact number, but from what I can tell, it’s been more or less the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have noticed that some, especially around work. It’s not every single night, but on nights before I’ve got a run the next day, I’ll get into bed and then my mind starts going over all the stops, what time I need to be where, whether traffic’s going to be bad, whether I’m forgetting something… just all that stuff.\n\nSometimes I do fall asleep eventually, but then I’ll wake up around four in the morning, maybe even a little before, and once I’m awake it’s like my brain turns back on. I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I know everybody thinks about death once in a while, especially getting older—you hear about someone getting sick, or there’s an accident on the road, that kind of thing, and it crosses your mind.

But if you mean have I been sitting around thinking about dying a lot, or feeling like I want to die, I don’t think so. Nothing like a plan or anything like that. I wouldn’t say that’s been a regular thing for me.

Sometimes I might have a passing thought like, “What’s the point of all this?” when I’m really worn out, but it’s more like frustration or being tired than actually wanting to be dead. I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking. So… no, not in a serious way, I guess.

From the outside I probably look like I’m managing. But inside it’s just this heavy, flat kind of feeling, like I’m not really in my own life the way I used to be. It’s not every single night, but on nights before I’ve got a run the next day, I’ll get into bed and then my mind starts going over all the stops, what time I need to be where, whether traffic’s going to be bad, whether I’m forgetting something… just all that stuff.\n\nSometimes I do fall asleep eventually, but then I’ll wake up around four in the morning, maybe even a little before, and once I’m awake it’s like my brain turns back on. I might still lie there for another hour or two, but I’m not really sleeping again. I’m just kind of watching the clock and thinking, well, if I fall asleep now I’ll get this much time, and then that makes it worse.\n\nI still usually make it to work on time, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m managing it okay. But those mornings, I feel like I’m already starting the day worn down before I’ve even gotten in the truck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I’m completely unable to feel pleasure, no. Like I can still have moments where I laugh at something, or I’m glad I did something instead of just sitting around. But if you mean whether the enjoyment feels kind of weaker or harder to get to than it used to, then yeah, I guess that’s closer.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I know everybody thinks about death once in a while, especially getting older—you hear about someone getting sick, or there’s an accident on the road, that kind of thing, and it crosses your mind.\n\nBut if you mean have I been sitting around thinking about dying a lot, or feeling like I want to die, I don’t think so. Nothing like a plan or anything like that. I wouldn’t say that’s been a regular thing for me.\n\nSometimes I might have a passing thought like, “What’s the point of all this?” when I’m really worn out, but it’s more like frustration or being tired than actually wanting to be dead. So… no, not in a serious way, I guess.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever found yourself wishing you were dead when you're feeling really down?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 50 / driver_sales_worker_or_truck_driver / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not in the sense of actually wishing I was dead. When I’m really down, I can get kind of fed up and think things like, “I’m tired of this,” or “I don’t want to deal with today,” but that’s different from wanting to be dead, at least in my mind.

It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while, or wanting a break from feeling worn out. I know that might sound close, but it doesn’t feel like I’m picturing death or hoping for it. I’m usually just frustrated, tired, and maybe feeling like I don’t have much left in the tank that day.

So… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve found myself wishing I were dead. More like wishing I could shut everything off for a bit and not have to think.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I’m completely unable to feel pleasure, no. That sounds a little too absolute for what it’s like for me.\n\nIt’s more that it takes more for something to feel enjoyable, or the enjoyment doesn’t last as long. Like I can still have moments where I laugh at something, or I’m glad I did something instead of just sitting around. But a lot of the time, things I used to look forward to feel more like… I don’t know, just something to get through, or something I do because I know I should.\n\nSo if you mean, do I never enjoy anything anymore, then no, that’s not exactly it. But if you mean whether the enjoyment feels kind of weaker or harder to get to than it used to, then yeah, I guess that’s closer.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I know everybody thinks about death once in a while, especially getting older—you hear about someone getting sick, or there’s an accident on the road, that kind of thing, and it crosses your mind.\n\nBut if you mean have I been sitting around thinking about dying a lot, or feeling like I want to die, I don’t think so. Nothing like a plan or anything like that. I wouldn’t say that’s been a regular thing for me.\n\nSometimes I might have a passing thought like, “What’s the point of all this?” when I’m really worn out, but it’s more like frustration or being tired than actually wanting to be dead. I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking. So… no, not in a serious way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself wishing you were dead when you're feeling really down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of actually wishing I was dead. When I’m really down, I can get kind of fed up and think things like, “I’m tired of this,” or “I don’t want to deal with today,” but that’s different from wanting to be dead, at least in my mind.\n\nIt’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while, or wanting a break from feeling worn out. I know that might sound close, but it doesn’t feel like I’m picturing death or hoping for it. I’m usually just frustrated, tired, and maybe feeling like I don’t have much left in the tank that day.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve found myself wishing I were dead. More like wishing I could shut everything off for a bit and not have to think.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 50 / driver_sales_worker_or_truck_driver / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I don’t think I have. Not harming myself, like actually doing something to myself or thinking through how I would do it. That’s not really where my mind goes.

I can get pretty low and worn out, and sometimes I’m angry at myself or frustrated, but I haven’t had thoughts like, “I should hurt myself,” or anything like that. I don’t keep anything in mind or have some plan tucked away. It’s more just feeling overwhelmed and wanting the day to be over, if that makes sense.

So, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had thoughts of harming myself.

From the outside I probably look like I’m managing. But inside it’s just this heavy, flat kind of feeling, like I’m not really in my own life the way I used to be. I’m getting things done, but I don’t feel very present in any of it. That’s probably the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the same things I used to do, and I can’t say I never enjoy anything at all, but it’s harder for me to tell what “pleasure” is supposed to feel like anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll go through the motions with things, and sometimes I’ll think, okay, that was fine, or I’m glad I did it instead of just sitting around. But it’s not always that same kind of easy enjoyment I remember having before. I guess I haven’t really sat down and measured it, you know? It’s more like I notice after the fact that things feel kind of muted. But I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely lost interest or anything that clear-cut.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this low feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three years, I’d say. It started after my father died, or at least that’s when I first really noticed it settling in.\n\nAt the time I thought, well, of course I feel bad, you know? That made sense. I figured it was just part of dealing with him being gone, and that it would lift after a while. But it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed there in the background, and then over time it became hard to tell where the grief ended and where this ongoing low mood began. So, yeah… roughly three years now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not sure I’d call it “lost interest,” exactly. I haven’t stopped doing everything I used to do, and there are still moments where something feels okay or worthwhile. It’s more like the feeling I get from things is dulled down.\n\nLike, if I do something I used to enjoy, I might still show up and participate, and afterward I can say, “Yeah, that was fine,” but I don’t always have that same pull toward it or that easy enjoyment while I’m doing it. Sometimes I’m doing it because it’s part of my routine, or because I know it’s probably better than doing nothing, rather than because I’m really looking forward to it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have zero interest, but it doesn’t feel the same as it used to. It’s kind of muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve clearly noticed, no. I mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little like anyone’s does, depending on what I’m eating or how busy I am with work, but I can’t point to some major change over the last three years.\n\nI haven’t had to buy a whole different size of clothes or anything like that, and nobody’s really commented on it to me. I don’t weigh myself all the time, so I couldn’t give you an exact number, but from what I can tell, it’s been more or less the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have noticed that some, especially around work. It’s not every single night, but on nights before I’ve got a run the next day, I’ll get into bed and then my mind starts going over all the stops, what time I need to be where, whether traffic’s going to be bad, whether I’m forgetting something… just all that stuff.\n\nSometimes I do fall asleep eventually, but then I’ll wake up around four in the morning, maybe even a little before, and once I’m awake it’s like my brain turns back on. I might still lie there for another hour or two, but I’m not really sleeping again. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Completely unenjoyable? I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I mean, there are definitely times where I don’t get as much out of things as I think I used to, or I kind of have to push myself to get going, but “completely” makes it sound like there’s just nothing there at all.

I still can enjoy things sometimes, at least a little. It might take me longer to settle into it, or I might not be as excited beforehand as I used to be, but once I’m doing something familiar or something easy, I can still get some enjoyment out of it. It’s not like everything has gone flat all the time.

So… no, I wouldn’t say things I used to enjoy are now completely unenjoyable. Maybe less enjoyable at times, but not completely gone.

I still get up, I still do my routes, I still show up for my volunteer shifts and do what I’m supposed to do, but most days it feels like I’m just kind of moving through it all on autopilot.\n\nIt’s not like I’m falling apart in some obvious way, I don’t think. From the outside I probably look like I’m managing. But inside it’s just this heavy, flat kind of feeling, like I’m not really in my own life the way I used to be. I’m getting things done, but I don’t feel very present in any of it. That’s probably the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the same things I used to do, and I can’t say I never enjoy anything at all, but it’s harder for me to tell what “pleasure” is supposed to feel like anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll go through the motions with things, and sometimes I’ll think, okay, that was fine, or I’m glad I did it instead of just sitting around. But it’s not always that same kind of easy enjoyment I remember having before. I guess I haven’t really sat down and measured it, you know? It’s more like I notice after the fact that things feel kind of muted. But I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely lost interest or anything that clear-cut.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this low feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three years, I’d say. It started after my father died, or at least that’s when I first really noticed it settling in.\n\nAt the time I thought, well, of course I feel bad, you know? That made sense. I figured it was just part of dealing with him being gone, and that it would lift after a while. But it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed there in the background, and then over time it became hard to tell where the grief ended and where this ongoing low mood began. So, yeah… roughly three years now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not sure I’d call it “lost interest,” exactly. I haven’t stopped doing everything I used to do, and there are still moments where something feels okay or worthwhile. It’s more like the feeling I get from things is dulled down.\n\nLike, if I do something I used to enjoy, I might still show up and participate, and afterward I can say, “Yeah, that was fine,” but I don’t always have that same pull toward it or that easy enjoyment while I’m doing it. Sometimes I’m doing it because it’s part of my routine, or because I know it’s probably better than doing nothing, rather than because I’m really looking forward to it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have zero interest, but it doesn’t feel the same as it used to. It’s kind of muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve clearly noticed, no. I mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little like anyone’s does, depending on what I’m eating or how busy I am with work, but I can’t point to some major change over the last three years.\n\nI haven’t had to buy a whole different size of clothes or anything like that, and nobody’s really commented on it to me. I don’t weigh myself all the time, so I couldn’t give you an exact number, but from what I can tell, it’s been more or less the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have noticed that some, especially around work. It’s not every single night, but on nights before I’ve got a run the next day, I’ll get into bed and then my mind starts going over all the stops, what time I need to be where, whether traffic’s going to be bad, whether I’m forgetting something… just all that stuff.\n\nSometimes I do fall asleep eventually, but then I’ll wake up around four in the morning, maybe even a little before, and once I’m awake it’s like my brain turns back on. I might still lie there for another hour or two, but I’m not really sleeping again. I’m just kind of watching the clock and thinking, well, if I fall asleep now I’ll get this much time, and then that makes it worse.\n\nI still usually make it to work on time, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m managing it okay. But those mornings, I feel like I’m already starting the day worn down before I’ve even gotten in the truck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I’m completely unable to feel pleasure, no. That sounds a little too absolute for what it’s like for me.\n\nIt’s more that it takes more for something to feel enjoyable, or the enjoyment doesn’t last as long. Like I can still have moments where I laugh at something, or I’m glad I did something instead of just sitting around. But a lot of the time, things I used to look forward to feel more like… I don’t know, just something to get through, or something I do because I know I should.\n\nSo if you mean, do I never enjoy anything anymore, then no, that’s not exactly it. But if you mean whether the enjoyment feels kind of weaker or harder to get to than it used to, then yeah, I guess that’s closer.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. It’s more just feeling overwhelmed and wanting the day to be over, if that makes sense.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had thoughts of harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things you used to enjoy are now completely unenjoyable to you?\n\nPatient: Completely unenjoyable? I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I mean, there are definitely times where I don’t get as much out of things as I think I used to, or I kind of have to push myself to get going, but “completely” makes it sound like there’s just nothing there at all.\n\nI still can enjoy things sometimes, at least a little. It might take me longer to settle into it, or I might not be as excited beforehand as I used to be, but once I’m doing something familiar or something easy, I can still get some enjoyment out of it. It’s not like everything has gone flat all the time.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say things I used to enjoy are now completely unenjoyable. Maybe less enjoyable at times, but not completely gone.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had more than one period of significant depression in the past three years?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it separate “periods” exactly, because it hasn’t felt like it cleanly came and went. It’s more like this whole thing has been going on for about three years now, really since my father died.

There have been stretches where it feels heavier and stretches where I’m functioning better, but it’s kind of been hanging around in the background that whole time. So if you mean, have I had more than one episode where I dropped down and then came back up completely and then dropped again, I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. But the depression, or whatever you want to call it, has been there in some form for about three years.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F33.0, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is that I’ve had this low feeling hanging around for a long time now. I still get up, I still do my routes, I still show up for my volunteer shifts and do what I’m supposed to do, but most days it feels like I’m just kind of moving through it all on autopilot.\n\nIt’s not like I’m falling apart in some obvious way, I don’t think. From the outside I probably look like I’m managing. But inside it’s just this heavy, flat kind of feeling, like I’m not really in my own life the way I used to be. I’m getting things done, but I don’t feel very present in any of it. That’s probably the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the same things I used to do, and I can’t say I never enjoy anything at all, but it’s harder for me to tell what “pleasure” is supposed to feel like anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll go through the motions with things, and sometimes I’ll think, okay, that was fine, or I’m glad I did it instead of just sitting around. But it’s not always that same kind of easy enjoyment I remember having before. I guess I haven’t really sat down and measured it, you know? It’s more like I notice after the fact that things feel kind of muted. But I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely lost interest or anything that clear-cut.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this low feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three years, I’d say. It started after my father died, or at least that’s when I first really noticed it settling in.\n\nAt the time I thought, well, of course I feel bad, you know? That made sense. I figured it was just part of dealing with him being gone, and that it would lift after a while. But it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed there in the background, and then over time it became hard to tell where the grief ended and where this ongoing low mood began. So, yeah… roughly three years now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not sure I’d call it “lost interest,” exactly. I haven’t stopped doing everything I used to do, and there are still moments where something feels okay or worthwhile. It’s more like the feeling I get from things is dulled down.\n\nLike, if I do something I used to enjoy, I might still show up and participate, and afterward I can say, “Yeah, that was fine,” but I don’t always have that same pull toward it or that easy enjoyment while I’m doing it. Sometimes I’m doing it because it’s part of my routine, or because I know it’s probably better than doing nothing, rather than because I’m really looking forward to it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have zero interest, but it doesn’t feel the same as it used to. It’s kind of muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve clearly noticed, no. I mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little like anyone’s does, depending on what I’m eating or how busy I am with work, but I can’t point to some major change over the last three years.\n\nI haven’t had to buy a whole different size of clothes or anything like that, and nobody’s really commented on it to me. I don’t weigh myself all the time, so I couldn’t give you an exact number, but from what I can tell, it’s been more or less the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have noticed that some, especially around work. It’s not every single night, but on nights before I’ve got a run the next day, I’ll get into bed and then my mind starts going over all the stops, what time I need to be where, whether traffic’s going to be bad, whether I’m forgetting something… just all that stuff.\n\nSometimes I do fall asleep eventually, but then I’ll wake up around four in the morning, maybe even a little before, and once I’m awake it’s like my brain turns back on. I might still lie there for another hour or two, but I’m not really sleeping again. I’m just kind of watching the clock and thinking, well, if I fall asleep now I’ll get this much time, and then that makes it worse.\n\nI still usually make it to work on time, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m managing it okay. But those mornings, I feel like I’m already starting the day worn down before I’ve even gotten in the truck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I’m completely unable to feel pleasure, no. That sounds a little too absolute for what it’s like for me.\n\nIt’s more that it takes more for something to feel enjoyable, or the enjoyment doesn’t last as long. Like I can still have moments where I laugh at something, or I’m glad I did something instead of just sitting around. But a lot of the time, things I used to look forward to feel more like… I don’t know, just something to get through, or something I do because I know I should.\n\nSo if you mean, do I never enjoy anything anymore, then no, that’s not exactly it. But if you mean whether the enjoyment feels kind of weaker or harder to get to than it used to, then yeah, I guess that’s closer.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I know everybody thinks about death once in a while, especially getting older—you hear about someone getting sick, or there’s an accident on the road, that kind of thing, and it crosses your mind.\n\nBut if you mean have I been sitting around thinking about dying a lot, or feeling like I want to die, I don’t think so. Nothing like a plan or anything like that. I wouldn’t say that’s been a regular thing for me.\n\nSometimes I might have a passing thought like, “What’s the point of all this?” when I’m really worn out, but it’s more like frustration or being tired than actually wanting to be dead. I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking. So… no, not in a serious way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself wishing you were dead when you're feeling really down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of actually wishing I was dead. When I’m really down, I can get kind of fed up and think things like, “I’m tired of this,” or “I don’t want to deal with today,” but that’s different from wanting to be dead, at least in my mind.\n\nIt’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while, or wanting a break from feeling worn out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really in the sense of feeling “back to normal” for a while. It’s been more… uneven, I guess. This has been going on for about three years, since my father died, and there have been better days or better stretches, sure, where I’m getting through work and handling things and maybe not feeling it as strongly.

But I don’t remember a time in those three years where I thought, “Okay, that’s over, I feel like myself again,” and then later it came back. It’s more like it’s stayed around in the background the whole time, sometimes quieter and sometimes heavier. So I’ve had times that were somewhat better, but not really a clear break from it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is that I’ve had this low feeling hanging around for a long time now. I still get up, I still do my routes, I still show up for my volunteer shifts and do what I’m supposed to do, but most days it feels like I’m just kind of moving through it all on autopilot.\n\nIt’s not like I’m falling apart in some obvious way, I don’t think. From the outside I probably look like I’m managing. But inside it’s just this heavy, flat kind of feeling, like I’m not really in my own life the way I used to be. I’m getting things done, but I don’t feel very present in any of it. That’s probably the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the same things I used to do, and I can’t say I never enjoy anything at all, but it’s harder for me to tell what “pleasure” is supposed to feel like anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll go through the motions with things, and sometimes I’ll think, okay, that was fine, or I’m glad I did it instead of just sitting around. But it’s not always that same kind of easy enjoyment I remember having before. I guess I haven’t really sat down and measured it, you know? It’s more like I notice after the fact that things feel kind of muted. But I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely lost interest or anything that clear-cut.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this low feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three years, I’d say. It started after my father died, or at least that’s when I first really noticed it settling in.\n\nAt the time I thought, well, of course I feel bad, you know? That made sense. I figured it was just part of dealing with him being gone, and that it would lift after a while. But it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed there in the background, and then over time it became hard to tell where the grief ended and where this ongoing low mood began. So, yeah… roughly three years now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not sure I’d call it “lost interest,” exactly. I haven’t stopped doing everything I used to do, and there are still moments where something feels okay or worthwhile. It’s more like the feeling I get from things is dulled down.\n\nLike, if I do something I used to enjoy, I might still show up and participate, and afterward I can say, “Yeah, that was fine,” but I don’t always have that same pull toward it or that easy enjoyment while I’m doing it. Sometimes I’m doing it because it’s part of my routine, or because I know it’s probably better than doing nothing, rather than because I’m really looking forward to it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have zero interest, but it doesn’t feel the same as it used to. It’s kind of muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve clearly noticed, no. I mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little like anyone’s does, depending on what I’m eating or how busy I am with work, but I can’t point to some major change over the last three years.\n\nI haven’t had to buy a whole different size of clothes or anything like that, and nobody’s really commented on it to me. I don’t weigh myself all the time, so I couldn’t give you an exact number, but from what I can tell, it’s been more or less the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have noticed that some, especially around work. It’s not every single night, but on nights before I’ve got a run the next day, I’ll get into bed and then my mind starts going over all the stops, what time I need to be where, whether traffic’s going to be bad, whether I’m forgetting something… just all that stuff.\n\nSometimes I do fall asleep eventually, but then I’ll wake up around four in the morning, maybe even a little before, and once I’m awake it’s like my brain turns back on. I might still lie there for another hour or two, but I’m not really sleeping again. I’m just kind of watching the clock and thinking, well, if I fall asleep now I’ll get this much time, and then that makes it worse.\n\nI still usually make it to work on time, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m managing it okay. But those mornings, I feel like I’m already starting the day worn down before I’ve even gotten in the truck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I’m completely unable to feel pleasure, no. That sounds a little too absolute for what it’s like for me.\n\nIt’s more that it takes more for something to feel enjoyable, or the enjoyment doesn’t last as long. Like I can still have moments where I laugh at something, or I’m glad I did something instead of just sitting around. But a lot of the time, things I used to look forward to feel more like… I don’t know, just something to get through, or something I do because I know I should.\n\nSo if you mean, do I never enjoy anything anymore, then no, that’s not exactly it. But if you mean whether the enjoyment feels kind of weaker or harder to get to than it used to, then yeah, I guess that’s closer.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I know everybody thinks about death once in a while, especially getting older—you hear about someone getting sick, or there’s an accident on the road, that kind of thing, and it crosses your mind.\n\nBut if you mean have I been sitting around thinking about dying a lot, or feeling like I want to die, I don’t think so. Nothing like a plan or anything like that. I wouldn’t say that’s been a regular thing for me.\n\nSometimes I might have a passing thought like, “What’s the point of all this?” when I’m really worn out, but it’s more like frustration or being tired than actually wanting to be dead. I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking. So… no, not in a serious way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself wishing you were dead when you're feeling really down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of actually wishing I was dead. When I’m really down, I can get kind of fed up and think things like, “I’m tired of this,” or “I don’t want to deal with today,” but that’s different from wanting to be dead, at least in my mind.\n\nIt’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while, or wanting a break from feeling worn out. I know that might sound close, but it doesn’t feel like I’m picturing death or hoping for it. I’m usually just frustrated, tired, and maybe feeling like I don’t have much left in the tank that day.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve found myself wishing I were dead. More like wishing I could shut everything off for a bit and not have to think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have. Not harming myself, like actually doing something to myself or thinking through how I would do it. That’s not really where my mind goes.\n\nI can get pretty low and worn out, and sometimes I’m angry at myself or frustrated, but I haven’t had thoughts like, “I should hurt myself,” or anything like that. I don’t keep anything in mind or have some plan tucked away. It’s more just feeling overwhelmed and wanting the day to be over, if that makes sense.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had thoughts of harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things you used to enjoy are now completely unenjoyable to you?\n\nPatient: Completely unenjoyable? I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking. So… no, not in a serious way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself wishing you were dead when you're feeling really down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of actually wishing I was dead. When I’m really down, I can get kind of fed up and think things like, “I’m tired of this,” or “I don’t want to deal with today,” but that’s different from wanting to be dead, at least in my mind.\n\nIt’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while, or wanting a break from feeling worn out. I know that might sound close, but it doesn’t feel like I’m picturing death or hoping for it. I’m usually just frustrated, tired, and maybe feeling like I don’t have much left in the tank that day.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve found myself wishing I were dead. More like wishing I could shut everything off for a bit and not have to think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have. Not harming myself, like actually doing something to myself or thinking through how I would do it. That’s not really where my mind goes.\n\nI can get pretty low and worn out, and sometimes I’m angry at myself or frustrated, but I haven’t had thoughts like, “I should hurt myself,” or anything like that. I don’t keep anything in mind or have some plan tucked away. It’s more just feeling overwhelmed and wanting the day to be over, if that makes sense.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had thoughts of harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things you used to enjoy are now completely unenjoyable to you?\n\nPatient: Completely unenjoyable? I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I mean, there are definitely times where I don’t get as much out of things as I think I used to, or I kind of have to push myself to get going, but “completely” makes it sound like there’s just nothing there at all.\n\nI still can enjoy things sometimes, at least a little. It might take me longer to settle into it, or I might not be as excited beforehand as I used to be, but once I’m doing something familiar or something easy, I can still get some enjoyment out of it. It’s not like everything has gone flat all the time.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say things I used to enjoy are now completely unenjoyable. Maybe less enjoyable at times, but not completely gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period of significant depression in the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it separate “periods” exactly, because it hasn’t felt like it cleanly came and went. It’s more like this whole thing has been going on for about three years now, really since my father died.\n\nThere have been stretches where it feels heavier and stretches where I’m functioning better, but it’s kind of been hanging around in the background that whole time. So if you mean, have I had more than one episode where I dropped down and then came back up completely and then dropped again, I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. But the depression, or whatever you want to call it, has been there in some form for about three years.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the past three years where you felt significantly better for a while, before feeling depressed again?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of feeling “back to normal” for a while. It’s been more… uneven, I guess. This has been going on for about three years, since my father died, and there have been better days or better stretches, sure, where I’m getting through work and handling things and maybe not feeling it as strongly.\n\nBut I don’t remember a time in those three years where I thought, “Okay, that’s over, I feel like myself again,” and then later it came back. It’s more like it’s stayed around in the background the whole time, sometimes quieter and sometimes heavier. From the outside I probably look like I’m managing. But inside it’s just this heavy, flat kind of feeling, like I’m not really in my own life the way I used to be. I’m getting things done, but I don’t feel very present in any of it. That’s probably the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the same things I used to do, and I can’t say I never enjoy anything at all, but it’s harder for me to tell what “pleasure” is supposed to feel like anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll go through the motions with things, and sometimes I’ll think, okay, that was fine, or I’m glad I did it instead of just sitting around. But it’s not always that same kind of easy enjoyment I remember having before. I guess I haven’t really sat down and measured it, you know? It’s more like I notice after the fact that things feel kind of muted. But I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely lost interest or anything that clear-cut.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this low feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three years, I’d say. It started after my father died, or at least that’s when I first really noticed it settling in.\n\nAt the time I thought, well, of course I feel bad, you know? That made sense. I figured it was just part of dealing with him being gone, and that it would lift after a while. But it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed there in the background, and then over time it became hard to tell where the grief ended and where this ongoing low mood began. So, yeah… roughly three years now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not sure I’d call it “lost interest,” exactly. I haven’t stopped doing everything I used to do, and there are still moments where something feels okay or worthwhile. It’s more like the feeling I get from things is dulled down.\n\nLike, if I do something I used to enjoy, I might still show up and participate, and afterward I can say, “Yeah, that was fine,” but I don’t always have that same pull toward it or that easy enjoyment while I’m doing it. Sometimes I’m doing it because it’s part of my routine, or because I know it’s probably better than doing nothing, rather than because I’m really looking forward to it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have zero interest, but it doesn’t feel the same as it used to. It’s kind of muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve clearly noticed, no. I mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little like anyone’s does, depending on what I’m eating or how busy I am with work, but I can’t point to some major change over the last three years.\n\nI haven’t had to buy a whole different size of clothes or anything like that, and nobody’s really commented on it to me. I don’t weigh myself all the time, so I couldn’t give you an exact number, but from what I can tell, it’s been more or less the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have noticed that some, especially around work. It’s not every single night, but on nights before I’ve got a run the next day, I’ll get into bed and then my mind starts going over all the stops, what time I need to be where, whether traffic’s going to be bad, whether I’m forgetting something… just all that stuff.\n\nSometimes I do fall asleep eventually, but then I’ll wake up around four in the morning, maybe even a little before, and once I’m awake it’s like my brain turns back on. I might still lie there for another hour or two, but I’m not really sleeping again. I’m just kind of watching the clock and thinking, well, if I fall asleep now I’ll get this much time, and then that makes it worse.\n\nI still usually make it to work on time, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m managing it okay. But those mornings, I feel like I’m already starting the day worn down before I’ve even gotten in the truck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I’m completely unable to feel pleasure, no. That sounds a little too absolute for what it’s like for me.\n\nIt’s more that it takes more for something to feel enjoyable, or the enjoyment doesn’t last as long. Like I can still have moments where I laugh at something, or I’m glad I did something instead of just sitting around. But a lot of the time, things I used to look forward to feel more like… I don’t know, just something to get through, or something I do because I know I should.\n\nSo if you mean, do I never enjoy anything anymore, then no, that’s not exactly it. But if you mean whether the enjoyment feels kind of weaker or harder to get to than it used to, then yeah, I guess that’s closer.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I know everybody thinks about death once in a while, especially getting older—you hear about someone getting sick, or there’s an accident on the road, that kind of thing, and it crosses your mind.\n\nBut if you mean have I been sitting around thinking about dying a lot, or feeling like I want to die, I don’t think so. Nothing like a plan or anything like that. I wouldn’t say that’s been a regular thing for me.\n\nSometimes I might have a passing thought like, “What’s the point of all this?” when I’m really worn out, but it’s more like frustration or being tired than actually wanting to be dead. I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking. So… no, not in a serious way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself wishing you were dead when you're feeling really down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of actually wishing I was dead. When I’m really down, I can get kind of fed up and think things like, “I’m tired of this,” or “I don’t want to deal with today,” but that’s different from wanting to be dead, at least in my mind.\n\nIt’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while, or wanting a break from feeling worn out. I know that might sound close, but it doesn’t feel like I’m picturing death or hoping for it. I’m usually just frustrated, tired, and maybe feeling like I don’t have much left in the tank that day.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve found myself wishing I were dead. More like wishing I could shut everything off for a bit and not have to think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have. Not harming myself, like actually doing something to myself or thinking through how I would do it. That’s not really where my mind goes.\n\nI can get pretty low and worn out, and sometimes I’m angry at myself or frustrated, but I haven’t had thoughts like, “I should hurt myself,” or anything like that. I don’t keep anything in mind or have some plan tucked away. It’s more just feeling overwhelmed and wanting the day to be over, if that makes sense.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had thoughts of harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things you used to enjoy are now completely unenjoyable to you?\n\nPatient: Completely unenjoyable? I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I mean, there are definitely times where I don’t get as much out of things as I think I used to, or I kind of have to push myself to get going, but “completely” makes it sound like there’s just nothing there at all.\n\nI still can enjoy things sometimes, at least a little. It might take me longer to settle into it, or I might not be as excited beforehand as I used to be, but once I’m doing something familiar or something easy, I can still get some enjoyment out of it. It’s not like everything has gone flat all the time.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say things I used to enjoy are now completely unenjoyable. Maybe less enjoyable at times, but not completely gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period of significant depression in the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it separate “periods” exactly, because it hasn’t felt like it cleanly came and went. It’s more like this whole thing has been going on for about three years now, really since my father died.\n\nThere have been stretches where it feels heavier and stretches where I’m functioning better, but it’s kind of been hanging around in the background that whole time. So if you mean, have I had more than one episode where I dropped down and then came back up completely and then dropped again, I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. But the depression, or whatever you want to call it, has been there in some form for about three years.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the past three years where you felt significantly better for a while, before feeling depressed again?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of feeling “back to normal” for a while. It’s been more… uneven, I guess. This has been going on for about three years, since my father died, and there have been better days or better stretches, sure, where I’m getting through work and handling things and maybe not feeling it as strongly.\n\nBut I don’t remember a time in those three years where I thought, “Okay, that’s over, I feel like myself again,” and then later it came back. It’s more like it’s stayed around in the background the whole time, sometimes quieter and sometimes heavier. So I’ve had times that were somewhat better, but not really a clear break from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two or more distinct periods where you felt depressed, with some period of feeling normal in between?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not like that. I know I keep circling around it, but it’s hard to separate it into neat chunks. It’s been going on for about three years, since my father died, and I can’t really point to a period in the middle where I felt normal again and then later got depressed again.\n\nThere have been ups and downs, sure, but not two clear, separate periods with a normal stretch in between. It’s more like the same thing has been there the whole time, just stronger sometimes and quieter other times.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

When I’m really down, I can get kind of fed up and think things like, “I’m tired of this,” or “I don’t want to deal with today,” but that’s different from wanting to be dead, at least in my mind.\n\nIt’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while, or wanting a break from feeling worn out. I know that might sound close, but it doesn’t feel like I’m picturing death or hoping for it. I’m usually just frustrated, tired, and maybe feeling like I don’t have much left in the tank that day.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve found myself wishing I were dead. More like wishing I could shut everything off for a bit and not have to think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have. Not harming myself, like actually doing something to myself or thinking through how I would do it. That’s not really where my mind goes.\n\nI can get pretty low and worn out, and sometimes I’m angry at myself or frustrated, but I haven’t had thoughts like, “I should hurt myself,” or anything like that. I don’t keep anything in mind or have some plan tucked away. It’s more just feeling overwhelmed and wanting the day to be over, if that makes sense.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had thoughts of harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things you used to enjoy are now completely unenjoyable to you?\n\nPatient: Completely unenjoyable? I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. It’s been more… uneven, I guess. It’s more like it’s stayed around in the background the whole time, sometimes quieter and sometimes heavier. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess what brings me in is that I’ve had this low feeling hanging around for a long time now. I still get up, I still do my routes, I still show up for my volunteer shifts and do what I’m supposed to do, but most days it feels like I’m just kind of moving through it all on autopilot.\n\nIt’s not like I’m falling apart in some obvious way, I don’t think. From the outside I probably look like I’m managing. But inside it’s just this heavy, flat kind of feeling, like I’m not really in my own life the way I used to be. I’m getting things done, but I don’t feel very present in any of it. That’s probably the best way I can explain it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the same things I used to do, and I can’t say I never enjoy anything at all, but it’s harder for me to tell what “pleasure” is supposed to feel like anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll go through the motions with things, and sometimes I’ll think, okay, that was fine, or I’m glad I did it instead of just sitting around. But it’s not always that same kind of easy enjoyment I remember having before. I guess I haven’t really sat down and measured it, you know? It’s more like I notice after the fact that things feel kind of muted. But I don’t know if I’d say I’ve completely lost interest or anything that clear-cut.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this low feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about three years, I’d say. It started after my father died, or at least that’s when I first really noticed it settling in.\n\nAt the time I thought, well, of course I feel bad, you know? That made sense. I figured it was just part of dealing with him being gone, and that it would lift after a while. But it didn’t really lift. It just sort of stayed there in the background, and then over time it became hard to tell where the grief ended and where this ongoing low mood began. So, yeah… roughly three years now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m still not sure I’d call it “lost interest,” exactly. I haven’t stopped doing everything I used to do, and there are still moments where something feels okay or worthwhile. It’s more like the feeling I get from things is dulled down.\n\nLike, if I do something I used to enjoy, I might still show up and participate, and afterward I can say, “Yeah, that was fine,” but I don’t always have that same pull toward it or that easy enjoyment while I’m doing it. Sometimes I’m doing it because it’s part of my routine, or because I know it’s probably better than doing nothing, rather than because I’m really looking forward to it.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have zero interest, but it doesn’t feel the same as it used to. It’s kind of muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve clearly noticed, no. I mean, my weight probably goes up and down a little like anyone’s does, depending on what I’m eating or how busy I am with work, but I can’t point to some major change over the last three years.\n\nI haven’t had to buy a whole different size of clothes or anything like that, and nobody’s really commented on it to me. I don’t weigh myself all the time, so I couldn’t give you an exact number, but from what I can tell, it’s been more or less the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns over the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have noticed that some, especially around work. It’s not every single night, but on nights before I’ve got a run the next day, I’ll get into bed and then my mind starts going over all the stops, what time I need to be where, whether traffic’s going to be bad, whether I’m forgetting something… just all that stuff.\n\nSometimes I do fall asleep eventually, but then I’ll wake up around four in the morning, maybe even a little before, and once I’m awake it’s like my brain turns back on. I might still lie there for another hour or two, but I’m not really sleeping again. I’m just kind of watching the clock and thinking, well, if I fall asleep now I’ll get this much time, and then that makes it worse.\n\nI still usually make it to work on time, so from the outside it probably looks like I’m managing it okay. But those mornings, I feel like I’m already starting the day worn down before I’ve even gotten in the truck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I’m completely unable to feel pleasure, no. That sounds a little too absolute for what it’s like for me.\n\nIt’s more that it takes more for something to feel enjoyable, or the enjoyment doesn’t last as long. Like I can still have moments where I laugh at something, or I’m glad I did something instead of just sitting around. But a lot of the time, things I used to look forward to feel more like… I don’t know, just something to get through, or something I do because I know I should.\n\nSo if you mean, do I never enjoy anything anymore, then no, that’s not exactly it. But if you mean whether the enjoyment feels kind of weaker or harder to get to than it used to, then yeah, I guess that’s closer.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying over the past three years?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I know everybody thinks about death once in a while, especially getting older—you hear about someone getting sick, or there’s an accident on the road, that kind of thing, and it crosses your mind.\n\nBut if you mean have I been sitting around thinking about dying a lot, or feeling like I want to die, I don’t think so. Nothing like a plan or anything like that. I wouldn’t say that’s been a regular thing for me.\n\nSometimes I might have a passing thought like, “What’s the point of all this?” when I’m really worn out, but it’s more like frustration or being tired than actually wanting to be dead. I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking. So… no, not in a serious way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself wishing you were dead when you're feeling really down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of actually wishing I was dead. When I’m really down, I can get kind of fed up and think things like, “I’m tired of this,” or “I don’t want to deal with today,” but that’s different from wanting to be dead, at least in my mind.\n\nIt’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while, or wanting a break from feeling worn out. I know that might sound close, but it doesn’t feel like I’m picturing death or hoping for it. I’m usually just frustrated, tired, and maybe feeling like I don’t have much left in the tank that day.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve found myself wishing I were dead. More like wishing I could shut everything off for a bit and not have to think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I have. Not harming myself, like actually doing something to myself or thinking through how I would do it. That’s not really where my mind goes.\n\nI can get pretty low and worn out, and sometimes I’m angry at myself or frustrated, but I haven’t had thoughts like, “I should hurt myself,” or anything like that. I don’t keep anything in mind or have some plan tucked away. It’s more just feeling overwhelmed and wanting the day to be over, if that makes sense.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had thoughts of harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things you used to enjoy are now completely unenjoyable to you?\n\nPatient: Completely unenjoyable? I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. I mean, there are definitely times where I don’t get as much out of things as I think I used to, or I kind of have to push myself to get going, but “completely” makes it sound like there’s just nothing there at all.\n\nI still can enjoy things sometimes, at least a little. It might take me longer to settle into it, or I might not be as excited beforehand as I used to be, but once I’m doing something familiar or something easy, I can still get some enjoyment out of it. It’s not like everything has gone flat all the time.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say things I used to enjoy are now completely unenjoyable. Maybe less enjoyable at times, but not completely gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period of significant depression in the past three years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it separate “periods” exactly, because it hasn’t felt like it cleanly came and went. It’s more like this whole thing has been going on for about three years now, really since my father died.\n\nThere have been stretches where it feels heavier and stretches where I’m functioning better, but it’s kind of been hanging around in the background that whole time. So if you mean, have I had more than one episode where I dropped down and then came back up completely and then dropped again, I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. But the depression, or whatever you want to call it, has been there in some form for about three years.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times in the past three years where you felt significantly better for a while, before feeling depressed again?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of feeling “back to normal” for a while. It’s been more… uneven, I guess. This has been going on for about three years, since my father died, and there have been better days or better stretches, sure, where I’m getting through work and handling things and maybe not feeling it as strongly.\n\nBut I don’t remember a time in those three years where I thought, “Okay, that’s over, I feel like myself again,” and then later it came back. It’s more like it’s stayed around in the background the whole time, sometimes quieter and sometimes heavier. So I’ve had times that were somewhat better, but not really a clear break from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had two or more distinct periods where you felt depressed, with some period of feeling normal in between?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not like that. I know I keep circling around it, but it’s hard to separate it into neat chunks. It’s been going on for about three years, since my father died, and I can’t really point to a period in the middle where I felt normal again and then later got depressed again.\n\nThere have been ups and downs, sure, but not two clear, separate periods with a normal stretch in between. It’s more like the same thing has been there the whole time, just stronger sometimes and quieter other times.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt completely better, without any depression, that lasted for at least a couple of months at any point in the past three years?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had a couple of months where it was just completely gone. I’ve had better stretches, like I said, where I could get through the day and maybe it wasn’t sitting on me so hard, but I wouldn’t say I was completely better.\n\nThis has pretty much been going on for about three years, since my father died. It’s not always the same level every day, but I can’t point to any two-month stretch where I felt totally normal and free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt completely free of depression since your father died?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think so. Since my father died, this has just kind of been going on the whole time — about three years now. There might’ve been days or even little stretches where I was managing better, where I wasn’t thinking about it every minute, but I can’t say there was ever a full two months where I felt completely free of it. It’s more like it’s been there in the background the whole time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It’s more like it’s been there in the background the whole time.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period of significant improvement where you felt like your old self again, even if it didn't last?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },
